# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I (Chapters 151–160), "One Box" — COMPLETE

**`outline/volume-04.md` was written in the same run as this batch. The load-book run is 154 to 163, continuous, one entry per chapter, no duplicate and no gap. Eight of the ten entries are about somebody who is not Marek Senn and the two that are about him are the choice (160) and the close (163).**

### Chapter 0151 — "The First Name In It"

**The Wednesday morning of week 47, from about ten until half past eleven, on a counter on the ground floor of a building in Bower Street and in the corridor outside it. Load-book entry 154, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a counter with about two hundred forms on it that nobody had collected, a box in pencil with a name in it, and a woman of thirty-eight who telephoned a building she had never been inside and gave a name to a stranger in about nine seconds and then asked for it back; the four seconds in the same chapter are how long it took her to take the share off the bay, and the two must not be confused.** The second Wednesday the box has been on the counter and it was empty on both. Four fields, one of them a tick, and on the top page of the first stack one box about the size of a stamp ruled in pencil with three words over it in a clerk's hand: *who ended it*. No reference number, nothing typed, not on a system, and the only box in this city for the person who removed a share. **Petra Lindqvist, 53**, nineteen years at that counter, told Ianthe Roke she has got four stacks and one of them is different and she has been looking at it since the spring, and that that is not a decision either. **The telephone rings and the woman on the other end has had one of the district's pages in a drawer at work since before the spring and has moved house twice with it in a drawer at work. She is Doreen Abbiss, thirty-eight, a night care worker. Four of them were on a bay, one of them answered, it was not her, she took it off anyway in four seconds, and a bay went off eleven minutes later and nobody was hurt. She was asked one question — were you willing — and did not answer it, and said instead: put my name in it.** It went in in pencil, in the size of a stamp, in her own hand, with the time said out loud. **Then she asked for it out, and was told in nine words that a name written in pencil is a name on a page in a building, and that the page goes back in a stack of about two hundred, and a district picks those up and nobody knows when, and that tearing it out makes a hole in a stack and a hole in a stack is a thing somebody asks about.** She said she had made a phone call and not a mistake, and that nobody asked her whether she wanted to put her name on anything and she would have said yes to being asked. The clerk said she has not been told what the box is for and is not going to ask, and that everything that has gone out of that counter in nineteen years was better printed than that page and not one of them was any use to the person who took it away, and that in about four years somebody in a district is going to lift that rubber band and read a woman's name and is not going to know that she was asked whether she was willing and did not answer it. **And at the end Doreen Abbiss said there is one other place in this city where she could put her name in her own words, it meets on a Wednesday, it is four minutes an item, and she will not go, because a caller signs their own words and the last time she signed anything in this city somebody found her.** Marek Senn was in the corridor for about forty minutes with a docket and could not hear a word of any of it, and did not knock and did not ask, and at ten past eleven he took his page and noticed the rubber band on the first stack was tighter than it had been that morning and did not think about it for about a week.

### Chapter 0152 — "Forty-One Lines On A Sheet"

**The Thursday morning of week 47 and the Thursday of week 48, on four landings in four buildings and at the top of a set of stairs. Load-book entry 155, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a woman of forty-six with a trolley and a bucket and four cloths over the side, and forty-one lines on a sheet of which one is a space for conditions where there are no conditions.** The condition walk has gone round four buildings every Thursday for nineteen years and the sheet has forty-one lines and nobody has ever put anything in the forty-first. **Ines Kolar, 46**, cardigan with pens, clipboard that is not a clipboard any more, told him there is no visitor column and that is not hers and she has never broken one and two men in nineteen years have been annoyed about it. She read him eleven weeks of the sheet on a landing instead, which cost her nine minutes of a morning she does not have, and he wrote eleven lines of 2-14 in the margin of a docket and got the ninth one wrong, and she caught it: that one is 2-11 and it has been 2-11 since the summer and you have written two fourteen because the one before it was two fourteen. **And she said the reason she told him he had got it wrong: she has been putting that sheet in a ring file for nineteen years and has never had to look at a plate to know what it says, and he is the first person in nineteen years who has wanted to write them down, and she was telling him that about herself and not as a criticism of him.** He then asked her the question that turned the morning — what happens if the plate is wrong, who checks, the sheet is made of the list — and she made him say it in a different order and said she was going to think about it for a week. **And then she told him about the plates: there are two kinds. A building plate is enamel, a number and nothing else, one in a hallway in four years, and nobody replaces it because it is not anybody's job. A service plate is printed, with the company's name along the bottom edge the size of her thumb, and the number on it is a service number, and the company lists every room they have ever put a plate on.** She has known for four years, from a man of fifty-five in a basement while he was doing something to a pipe, and has never said it. **The plate in Bower Street is a service plate and the room is 2-07 and has been since before she came, and about nine hundred doors in four buildings are the same, and both numbers are correct about themselves and neither is the building's. About four hundred consent leaflets in the rack all say 2-07, and the room on a leaflet is the room on the plan and not the room on the door, and reconciling the two is not a condition.** She said there is no visitor column and he is not coming on the round, and that he is going to walk four buildings with her on Thursday mornings, and the sheet will say one name because the sheet says one name, and if anybody asks him why he was in the second building he is to say he was waiting for a door to open, and that will be true and will be a worse answer than the truth and is the one that keeps him out of a file. **On the Thursday of week 48 she walked the wall instead of the list for eleven minutes on the fourth building, found a plate saying 2-03 where the list said 2-09, put the clipboard on a windowsill, and said the thing she had been carrying since the Tuesday: there is nowhere to put it. A discrepancy is not a condition, and a condition is the only thing that sheet takes, and the moment it goes in somebody signs the bottom of that page and the signature means the room is as the list says it is and it is not.** She said she has been a person who has somewhere to put a thing for nineteen years and has run out for the first time, and has not slept properly since Tuesday, and is forty-six. **There is no form in this city that takes a discrepancy.**

### Chapter 0153 — "Nine In Date"

**The Friday of week 47, from a canteen off a service road at ten past one to a car park at half past two, on grade three. Load-book entry 156, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a man of thirty-four who works nights, who has two cards in one wallet, who has stopped telling people that the other sort is a front, and who came and had nothing to bring.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, the eleven parts said eleven times, the word at nine, ten and eleven a minute, the count on a docket in an inside pocket next to a piece of card about two inches by three. **The man of thirty-four said he has stopped saying that anybody handing you the other sort is a front, three weeks ago, and not because he was told to and not because the other one is worse, but because he said it to a man in a doorway in Nine Bridges in the spring and went back before the summer was out and the man had gone and the doorway was a stairwell for a different building, and he has been stood in about four stairwells since.** He said it was a worse reason for the case and a better one for him. **He has two cards and does not know which one to do the job with, and has not rung anybody because there is no number to ring: the card says if no word, withdraw and report, the word has not come for about four months, and there is nobody to report to. He has rung a company's service line four times in four months, about forty minutes of it in total, and has been told to ring the district.** He has counted about nine on a Friday twice since the spring and once since the summer, and said twice is a sample of two, and that if the card is wrong then four people a week are standing in rooms waiting for a person who is not on the rota, and that he has been one of those twice and there was nobody there and he went home both times and did the job anyway, and that doing it anyway is not a system. He said he came and had nothing to bring and that that was the whole of it. **The four-minute briefing: a district telephoned at ten past one with a written question, and the depot manager of fifty-two read it out because it is a written question and not a conversation. It wanted to know whether a name could be put against a release on a Friday and whether the name would be the person who gave the word. He said nine words — a release is finished by the person holding it, not by a person waiting — and somebody at the end of the table wrote it down. And then the depot manager said, to nobody in particular, that the condition stops him doing the thing and does not stop anybody asking him about the thing, and that there is no rule in this company or in this city about who may be asked, and that he has checked it three times and the answer is that there is not one.** **And then the word did the new thing: he tried to write down two sentences that had mattered, in the order they had been said in, and could not, and wrote them in the wrong order, and the wrong order made a man who had come to help sound like a man who had come to complain. He crossed it out and wrote a note on the docket: cannot hold the order of two sentences after two o'clock, not the words, the order, ask him again or do not use it. And then decided the note is not right either.**

### Chapter 0154 — "A Card Catalogue With A Rule"

**The Monday morning of week 48, in a public reading room in Lattice Ward from about nine to twenty past eleven. Load-book entry 157, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a woman of forty-one who works three days a week and has said a rule out loud in eleven words and has said the other thing twice on purpose.** Four things on a slip: what you want, from when, to when, who is asking. A counter with a bell, four drawers of about four hundred cards, a trolley with a high shelf. **Wendla Ossick, 41**, Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays, thirty-one years in the building and nine in the room, gave the rule inside eleven seconds: a box does not come down without a slip, and a slip is a piece of paper and a person; it was a rule before she came, and nobody argues with it, and that is not a compliment, it is a Tuesday. **He filled one in and she did not touch it: the Marrow Falls material — there is no box. Nothing was taken out of that room; things were taken out of rooms. Four volumes went down a corridor in the week of week thirty-eight, fourteen years ago, to a room in another building with a register, and the register has a line on it with a date and a hand and a place, and if you want the binder you do not want a box from her, you want a line in a register in Crown Terrace. And then she said *nothing is sealed* twice, deliberately, the second time watching his face: there is no lock in that room and there has never been a lock in that room and there is a sign about eleven feet from the door that says so and nobody has read it, and what there is, is a column in a register in another building, and a column is not a lock, and a column can be read by anybody who can get into the building it is in, and that building has a counter on the ground floor and the register is on a table by the counter.** She said nobody does it because nobody has been in a hurry, and because a hand on a register looks like a person to ask, and there is not one. **He asked what happens if he leaves the last line off the slip, and she said it does not go in the tray — there is no tray — it comes back to him, because a slip with a person on it is a request and a slip without one is a piece of paper, and she has not got a tray for pieces of paper, and that is not her being difficult, that is nine years of a room with a counter and a bell.** He said there is no name in this city that ought to be on that slip and that he is the one that would go on it, and that he has spent a term and a half not doing that, and she said then do not do it. And then she said one thing once and would not say it twice: he is the first person in about four years who has told her what is actually at the end of it, because they all want a date, and he told her a corridor and a counter and a woman of thirty-eight. **At twenty past eleven he wrote down six of the shelf numbers off about four hundred cards and got one of the six wrong, and did not find out until the Wednesday of week fifty, and the chapter says plainly that the number was not the thing that mattered: a room like that is built out of about four hundred cards in four drawers, every one of them with a person who put it there written on it in a shorthand, and the reason anybody can find anything in there is that the finding is a card in a drawer, and the card is not hidden and not locked and is in a building anybody can walk into, and nobody has ever asked her to think about that, and he did not either until about six weeks later, by which time it was in a book in an inside pocket on a chair and it had cost somebody a name.** The name at the bottom of the slip is blank.

### Chapter 0155 — "A Smaller Question"

**The Tuesday afternoon of week 48, on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace and then in the corridor outside it. Load-book entry 158, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a man of fifty-eight in a coat that has never been in a workshop, and a woman of thirty-four at a table with a cup of tea, and a register in four columns with about nine hundred lines in it and not one name in the fourth column.** The register is bound in canvas, ruled into four columns, about nine hundred lines, going back fourteen years to the week of week thirty-eight, on a table by a counter, no rope and no sign. **A Continuity Office assessor of thirty-four reads it out to anybody who asks and said that before he asked, and that it is a public document and that is her whole view.** About four people in fourteen years have wanted a line about that week, two were told it was a commercial matter, and one of them was her. **She read him the column headings first because she always does, and the fourth one twice: date, place, hand, authority — and every one of about nine hundred lines in the authority column is a job title, and not one of them has ever been a person.** She has been at that table four years and has asked twice, once at four months in and been told it is how the register is written, and once when she did not ask. The hand column is blank in four lines in ten. **Iven Sore, 58**, soft voice, a coat that has never been in a workshop, asked three smaller questions before he sat down. **He refused, and said he was going to reward an honest sentence by refusing, and would not lead with the count because he would then think it was a negotiation. His reason is the best one anybody in this city has given for anything: after the outage about nine people read those pages who were not asked to, four wrote to him, three of those letters were printed, every one of the three wanted the pages at once, and every one of the three was wrong about a single thing, which is that the pages are not a route, because a route has a person on it who can say no and a set of pages is a set of pages. The third letter went to four hundred premises and nine people in this city are still getting letters about it.** He said that is a good reason and that he has had fourteen years to notice it is also a door, and the door is that a person who says the thing is a set of pages is a person who can be asked whether they said it. **The count: six lines relate to the binder, two of the about nine hundred were written by a hand rather than a title, and one of those two has an author still in this city, and he will not say which and will not say why, and said that if it is written down and put in front of a district nobody will be able to do anything with it and it will be a piece of paper about a man in a coat. A count is not a line and a count is not a person, and he has said that for four years to nine people and it has never once made him feel better. And then the one thing he did, which is not an opening: two offices have offered in writing to have the register moved into a locked room upstairs and he has declined both, because a locked room would have been the correct decision about the pages and the end of the corridor, and he would rather have a corridor that does not work than a locked room that does — and he would not say what on a piece of paper would be acceptable, because the first person who tells him what is acceptable on a piece of paper is the last person he will ever be able to say yes to anything.** The woman of thirty-four read him out to the door herself, and at the threshold said he had asked what was in the fourth column and not what she thought of it, that those are the same question and not the same question, that about four people in four years have asked her the first one, and that if he comes back she would like to be asked the first one. He asked, and she said she had not got one and was not going to make one up in a doorway.

### Chapter 0156 — "The Book Is Not A Register"

**The Wednesday of week 48, from eleven at night to about twenty past midnight, in a back room and on a half landing. Load-book entry 159, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a book at forty-four lines that was not opened, and a woman of sixty-one with a tin who gave nine minutes to the reason, and a woman of thirty-eight who would not sign her own words and then said them anyway to a room that wrote none of it down.** Nine people, a tin that is not locked, a four-column book at forty-four lines with the fortieth three years old and a hole in line forty-four where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four should have been, and a fifth chair against the wall that has been empty since the spring. Four minutes an item; the caller's own sentence in the first person with nothing changed and a signature at the bottom; no vote for anybody. **Bea Nunn, 61**, did not do the two minutes, put her hand flat on the book and said nobody has called, and when somebody said it is Wednesday she said it is Wednesday and it is eleven at night and nine people in a room is not nine callers, and that she has not opened the book in about six weeks and if she opens it tonight she will be writing down what somebody said in a room, and the rule of that room is that it is a book of what a caller said, and a caller is a person who rang, and she had thought about it for four days, which is longer than she has thought about anything in that room since the spring.** The one who could not sign was already sitting down. **Doreen Abbiss had come to a Wednesday and had read the leaflet and had not told anybody and had not brought anything, and said she had been carrying the name in the box for two days and had not told anybody she was carrying it, and was telling nine people because it is the only room in this city where nobody has a job in it.** Bea Nunn gave her the rule in one go: you can say it, all of it, in your own words, nobody will write it down and nobody will stop you and there is no rule in this room against talking; what there is a rule against is the book, because a caller is a person and a person signs their own words and a practice cannot sign anything, and that is the only reason this book is different from the sheet of typing paper three people wrote on four weeks ago and the only reason either of them exists. She said the woman could go in the book if she rang and was not going to, and would rather she did not, and would not ask why not because she had got nine minutes. **Then she said the reason, in eleven seconds, and it is a rule: a room of people who did not call is not nine callers, and a line in a book is a promise that somebody stood at a telephone and wanted something and got it, and if she writes tonight then in about four years somebody reads line forty-five and finds out that a woman of thirty-eight who did not want to be found said a true thing in a room with nine people in it, and that is not a book of callers, that is a book of people she liked.** She has liked four people in nine years, has written two down, and was wrong both times. **So Doreen Abbiss talked for nine minutes and none of it was written down: four of them on a bay, one of them answered and it was not her, four seconds, a bay eleven minutes later, nobody hurt, not slept since the Tuesday of week thirty-eight, four hundred and thirty-two cards in her coat from a printer of fifty-eight, a man of twenty-two with a lanyard who said in a corridor that a name on the front of a thing is the end of it, and that she put her own name on a page four days later anyway in about nine seconds on a telephone to a stranger. At about eight minutes she said she was not asking anybody for anything, and that was noted in the room and not in the book.** He was on the half landing and did not go up. **She came down past him and said she could hear somebody outside a door on Wednesday morning for about forty minutes and could not hear what they were saying and it did not matter at the time and matters now, and then gave him the thing she had not been able to say to anybody: a form with four fields and a tick is the best thing anybody in this city has written and it does not work, it does not work because of a box that was not on it, and now there is a box with her name in it and she cannot get it out, and nobody drew that box on purpose and nobody is going to fix it, and there is no post. And then nine words: the next time you want a name on a piece of paper, ask the person whose name it is, before you have got a pencil out.** Bea Nunn came out last and said she did not mind that he had not come up, and wanted it written down that she did not mind, because people come up for the wrong reasons and he has not got one.**

### Chapter 0157 — "Before The Pencil"

**The Thursday morning of week 48, from twenty past nine to about eleven, at a counter in a reading room in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 160, and it is about him, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a slip in four pieces in a bin by a bell, and nine words said by a woman of thirty-eight to a man who was about to use her, and it is the choice of this movement and he has not got a better sentence for it than the one she said first.** A slip with four things on it, the fourth one a person, and the pen in his hand. **Wendla Ossick said you are going to do it, and he said I am going to do something worse than do it, and she asked him to say what that is in the nine minutes she had before a school group, and he said: I am going to put somebody else's name on it, and she said I know, and I have watched you think about it on Monday.** The arithmetic: there are six lines in a register in Crown Terrace with a Wednesday and a hand on them, and a woman of thirty-four at a table who reads them out to anybody, and the address of that building is on a sign, and the address is in a district leaflet, and the district leaflet has a job title on it, and the job title is in about four hundred pay packets in one district — **so the one name he could put on a slip to make a paper version of himself, with a person at the bottom of it who could be asked who asked, is the woman whose name is in a box in pencil on a counter in Bower Street, whose district the register is about, and whose corridor is the corridor with about forty-one people on a link nobody can end.** He wrote the first two letters and did not write the third. **"Do not stop," said a woman behind him. Doreen Abbiss had been standing by the trolley for about four minutes and had read over his shoulder.** He said he was going to do it and then undo it and would like her standing there when he did. He said the reason he was going to tear it up is that a name on the front of that is the end of her being unfindable, and that she is the only person in this city who is unfindable and has a job, and that he has spent a term and a half not being findable and had spent four days working out how to spend it, and not on him. **She took the slip out of his hand, looked at the two letters, and said: you have just made me the reason.** Then she made him say what he meant, and gave the half he did not have: **a piece of paper with no name on it is a piece of paper somebody has to be angry about**, and that is what a room full of people does to it, and about four years after a man of forty-four wrote two words in two boxes in his own hand, four thousand went out to about eleven thousand premises and about forty people who had written in since the spring were told nobody was dealing with it, and she has that leaflet and everybody has that leaflet. **He put the pen down and asked her properly, before the pencil, as she had told him to on a half landing on Wednesday, and said that if she said no there would be no slip and no request and he would not come back to that counter about it, and that he had spent a fortnight asking a man of thirty-four to go round four buildings with a trolley and nobody had asked her anything yet.** She said yes, to the table, not to the reading room and not to a district and not to anybody with a form. **Her cost, before the yes: she works nights, six years, she is awake in the day, and being awake in the day is the only part of the week that is hers; and if she is a person who has been asked something then the next time somebody in this city needs a person it is her they come to, and she will come, and she will come in the day, and the daytime is six weeks of it and then it is not.** He said then say no. She said she had said yes, with the cost said out loud first, which is the way she was taught, and that nobody in six years has ever let her do it that way round, and that she wanted it noted that he did not try to talk her out of the cost once. **He tore the slip in half and then in four, on the counter, in front of the registrar, and put the pieces in the bin by the bell. She said: that is the correct action, and a request with a person on it and a reason on it is better than nothing, and you have thrown away something better than nothing, and I am not going to say anything else about it and I have a school group in eleven minutes.**

### Chapter 0158 — "Two Words In A Box"

**The Friday of week 48, from about half past six to a quarter to eleven, at the seventh of the eleven sessions in a room in Crown Terrace. Load-book entry 161, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is a woman of twenty-six who types things and has eleven letters in a folder behind her that she has not answered, and who stood up in a room of about forty people for the first time in three years.** Thirty-one folding chairs and two empty, a microphone, four hundred leaflets by the door with a fee on them, a session title and five jobs listed on the front, of which two are him, chosen by a system out of two job sheets in the week of the thirty-fourth. **The woman who types things is twenty-six, has been at the fund for three years, types the letters that go out, does not type the specification and does not sign the returns, and had eleven letters in a folder on a shelf behind her all addressed to a man of forty-four and all asking the same question in a different first line, and had answered none, and nobody had asked her to, and she said on the Thursday that she was not going to and that it is not a decision: she had decided there is nothing on the paper to answer them with.** The officer read the specification in the same voice and then the third line, which is not a section, and nine people did not hear the difference, and said the third line is nine words and is funded and is the only sentence anybody is going to read and that about four thousand went out with the last one. He said he was going to do something at the end that was not in his job and wanted it minuted, and nobody was minuting. **Three people asked him questions about one of the two cards in the first half hour and he answered in nine words each time, and a man in a district jacket in the fifth row wrote all three down — the standing problem, the fourth time, and not resolved.** **Peter Amado, about fifty-one, asked for the microphone to be switched off before he would say what he had come to say, for the second Friday running, and somebody switched it off, and he said the true thing and then said the other thing: that he does not know how long he can keep doing it, that nobody has offered him a way out, and that he is going to keep coming.** Somebody said he did not have to, and he said that is the fourth time in six Fridays, that he has thought about the offer more than he has thought about the fee, that he is going to take the fee anyway, and that in about four years nobody will be able to tell whether he believes it, and he would like it on a record that he said it while he still knew. **Then the woman of twenty-six stood up, which she has never done in three years, and said there are five fields on the front sheet, four are empty, one has a date in it, and the two that say no name are the only two anybody can read from the outside, because they are the only two with words in them, one in a man's handwriting and one in hers, and she did not write either of them, and every one of those eleven letters has been sent to a woman of twenty-six who types things.** She said she was not going to ask him to change it and did not turn round, and everybody in that room knew who she meant and not one of them said a name. She asked for two things and neither was a favour: that it be minuted that she had not been asked to do anything and had come, and that she is going back to a folder with eleven letters on Monday and is not going to answer them. And she said the other thing and sat down: about eleven people a week are handed a card that says a release waits for somebody, and there is no field on any of it that says who is going to be standing in the room when the word does not come, and she has looked. **And at a quarter to eleven the man of forty-four said the second half of his own sentence out loud for the first time in that room: a document with no name on it is a document people get angry at, and four years after he wrote two words in two boxes, four thousand pieces of paper went out to eleven thousand premises and about forty people who had written in since the spring were told nobody was dealing with it, and everybody in this city has that leaflet, and he has read it as carefully as she has.** Somebody asked which is it and he said both, and would not resolve it, and would not pretend the second half is a smaller version of the first, and listed what he has: a fund, eleven thousand premises, a specification with three lines, a committee of about nine that does not meet again, a leaflet in nine hundred rooms citing a company's own assurance as evidence, four hundred and thirty-two cards in about nine places in one district that nobody can take back, four months of letters about two words in two boxes' worth of handwriting, and the fact that he cannot stop any of it and knew about half of it a fortnight ago.

### Chapter 0159 — "Four Feet Of Shop, And The Money"

**The Monday of week 49, from about ten until half past five, in four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter. Load-book entry 162, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is four hundred cards with a shop's name on the back of them, a hundred and forty-one pounds going out of a shop in a bag, and a boy of nineteen who is not going to be on any of them with his name and is going to be on all of them with his handwriting.** The shop is four feet wide and has been for nineteen years and the sign has the owner's name on it in a typeface she paid for out of her own money in her first year. A boy of nineteen with his coat on. **A person in a wet coat who had not taken it off, which is a thing people do when they are not staying, wanted four hundred more of the cards with the fifth line on the back.** Sunniva Bellhouse, 58, said: say a number, say what you want on them, sit down, and I am going to say three things out loud and there are three people in this shop which is two more than there need to be. She did not ask him for a name and said it was not a courtesy — a form in this city wanted a contact and did not get one, and she has read about what happens to the people who fill those in, and the boy on that till is nineteen. **First: not off a photograph this time. Set from the same type, by a man who has never had a company's card in his hand in his life, and the customer said that is more money and she said it is slower and worse for her and right, and she has been fifty-eight for a long time. Second: the name of the shop goes on the back of every one of the four hundred, in the same place, the same way, the same size, the one on the sign and not a person — because the four hundred and thirty-two have her name in the postcode and none of them has her name on the card, and that was a decision she made in the spring in about nine minutes and it was the worst decision anybody has made in that shop in nineteen years and it was hers, and it means a card with that shop's name on it is a card a person can trace, and it is the only weapon she has got.** Third, from under the till, an envelope with a hundred and forty-one pounds in it and no name on it and no address on it, which had been there since the spring: **she gave it back. She said she was not being brave and not being clever and had thought about it for about three weeks and could not sit on it any more, and then said the cost out loud because she is paying it: in about four years, if anybody wants to know who printed four hundred and thirty-two cards, there is nothing on that table to take; the money is going out of the shop in a bag with a person; and if that person is the wrong person then the wrong person has got a hundred and forty-one pounds and her with it, and there is no version of the right thing that does not also do that.** The customer put it inside a coat and said nothing about it, which was correct. **Then she asked the boy to do the job in front of the customer and said he could say no and she would not be disappointed, and he asked why him and not her, and she said because she has made a decision about what a card is for and has not got the right to make a second one and he has, and if he makes the same one there are two of them and if he makes a different one she has to live with a card with his thinking on it, which is a thing she deserves. He said: I have been on this till about four months and I have never run the machine and everybody keeps telling me I have not run the machine, and I am going to do this and I am not going to be the one who did it in about four years, and I want it written down that I did it on a Monday morning and not on a Saturday, and that I am not going to be on any of them with my name and I am going to be on all of them with my handwriting. She said: that is the correct answer and it is worse than yours. He said yes. She said then do it.** **Petar Vance, Threadline field service engineer, came at about half past two because the shop is on a service list and the sign is on a service list, with a bag with a meter in it that was not out of order. He said he was not here about the cards: about nine hundred plates in four buildings carry his company's name along the bottom edge, he did not choose the numbers, they come off a room list that is not his, he has asked for it twice and been told it is the building's, and he cannot change about nine hundred doors because a district nurse wants one room, and that is not a complaint, that is a service, and he has said so in writing and been copied on a letter about it. The boy asked him the second question anybody in this city has asked: if the room list is not yours, whose is it. And he said the building's, and the building's is a set of plans in a basement in a drawer on a hook, and there has not been a person in that basement since about the spring, and he has the plates and the plans have the rooms, and the two of them have not met in nineteen years, and if you want to know which one is right the answer is that they are both right about themselves and one of them is in a drawer. And then he said that if anybody in that shop writes that down, he has not said it and he will deny it and he will be able to, because he has not said it.**

### Chapter 0160 — "The Back Of The Sheet"

**The Thursday of week 50, from about nine in the morning to about half past eleven, in four buildings and in a repair shop in Lattice Ward. Load-book entry 163, and it says in its own opening label that this entry is the back of a sheet, and fourteen, and a woman of forty-six telling him what the pencil is going to cost, and a corridor on Thursday of next week that he is not going to be able to be quiet in.** The forty-first line has something in it for the first time in nineteen years: **fourteen lines in pencil, on the back, upside down relative to the front, in the space where the conditions go, and it is not a condition.** Nine are in the building in Bower Street, three in the second building, and two in a stairwell in Saltmarket that a company plated in the year the stairwell was new and that are not mistakes, and she put them on the sheet anyway because a condition is a thing and this is a thing and if she does not write it down then in about four years there will be one number left in her head and she will be sixty-two and she will have moved house twice and one of them was to a smaller flat, and that is not a threat, it is a fact about how she would remember things. **The back of the sheet goes in the same ring file as the front, the ring file goes in a cupboard on a ground floor in another part of this city, and there are about nine hundred sheets in that file going back nineteen years, and there is no column for it, no box on it, and no form anywhere in this city that takes a discrepancy.** The fourth building has a set of plans in a drawer on a hook in a basement that nobody has stood in front of since the spring. **They did not go down. He wrote in his own book that nobody in this case has earned a basement, and that a basement is the room where the records are, and that if he is the first person through that door then he is the man who went through a door, and about eleven people a week are being taught to wait for that. Ines Kolar said at the top of the stairs that she had never been down and was not going today, and asked him to write it down as a thing she said and not a thing he found out.** **The price, said before he asked: in about four weeks a woman of thirty-six is going to come into that office and do the sheets up, because that is her job and she has done it for about nine years and does not know her, and she is going to turn that sheet over because everybody turns it over, and she is going to see fourteen lines in pencil in the space where the conditions go, and she is going to ask what the pencil is. And she will say there was a discrepancy and that she wrote it in pencil on her own sheet and that it has not been actioned, which is true and is nine words and does not help, because the woman of thirty-six will have to decide what to do with fourteen lines that a woman of forty-six wrote on the back of a sheet instead of in a column, and there is no column, and she will do whatever she does and he will not know. And when somebody asks her about the pencil she is going to have to say that a man of twenty-two asked her a question in a corridor in the week of forty-seventh that her sheet could not answer, and that is his name, and it is the only place his name is going to be in this city as a result of anything she has done, and she worked that out on the Monday and not on the Thursday.** **What it is worth, in her words, and he had not asked: two weeks ago a man could stand in that corridor and say the door and the wall do not agree and about four people in four buildings would have told him he was talking nonsense; in about four weeks there is a piece of paper in a ring file in a cupboard that says fourteen times, in a hand, that they do not; and that is worth about a fortnight, and in about a year it will be worth nothing, because the pencil will come off, and then the only thing anybody has got is a woman of forty-six who says it on a Thursday.** And then the price on the landing: **in four weeks the only thing that has changed in this city is a sheet with pencil on the back of it, and about eleven people a week are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, and he is still that man, and he did that on purpose in a room with nine people in it.** She said yes. He said he was not asking her to make it into more than it is, and she said she was not going to and was not going to pretend either, and then gave him the fortnight and the year. **The close, in the repair shop: five things in the drawer under the bench, four of them other people's sentences; a jacket over the back of the chair by the bench with a corner of card in the inside pocket of it, about two inches by three, which nobody has ever held out a hand for in a term and a half; a form four miles away with a box in pencil and a woman's name in it and no reference number and nothing typed and it cannot come out; about forty-one people on a thing that cannot be ended; about nine hundred doors a year off a company's list; plans in a drawer on a hook; six lines in a register whose fourth column has not had a person's name in it in fourteen years and a woman of thirty-four there who has not been asked what she thinks of that; and on the Thursday of next week a woman of thirty-eight who works nights is going to stand in that corridor with him while he asks it, and the answer will not be about him. There is no post and there is no office whose business it is to look at a stranger's wrist and there is not going to be one, and about eleven people a week are still waiting for a man in a lanyard that four weeks of work has not made unnecessary. Nobody has said so.**

---

# VOLUME 04 MOVEMENT I — THE CHAPTER SUMMARIES ABOVE ARE PRE-REPAIR TEXT AND ARE SUPERSEDED ON THE POINTS BELOW

**Three review and repair passes have been run on Chapters 151–160. **The ten summaries under `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I` above were written from the pre-repair text and every one of them is superseded on the points listed here. Everything else in them stands: the people, the rooms, the days, the quantities, the refusals and the entry numbers are correct as written.** Chapter 159 was not opened in the first pass and stands unaltered. This block replaces about 30 KB of running narrative; nothing was deleted from the repository, the full text of each pass is in the git history of this file and in `reviews/volume-04-batch-0001.md`, and the batch's own account is in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md`.**

1. **Chapter 151.** Two pages, not one: the page the box is for is in a drawer upstairs in Ianthe Roke's exchange room, four fields filled in from the Tuesday of week thirty-eight, and Doreen Abbiss's page is a different sheet in a drawer at work, hers saying a bay where that one says a room, and neither has a box on it. **Ianthe Roke's sentence to her is eleven words, not nine.** The entry's *two days before this* is a fortnight.
2. **Chapter 152.** The scene dated the Thursday of week forty-eight was cut and replaced with a plant; the chapter now ends on her reading the plate on every door before she reads anything off the list, stopping twice, and saying there is a thing she will not say until the next Thursday. **The scope is four buildings, about nine hundred rooms on four pages, forty-one doors a week, one sheet a week, forty-one lines, the forty-first a space for conditions where there are no conditions.** The two kinds of plate, the man of fifty-five in a basement about four years ago, and about four hundred leaflets in a rack that all say 2-07, are unchanged. **The 2-07 in Chapter 160 is the same chain: the room number on a leaflet is the room number on the plan, and it is the rack that the door disagrees with.**
3. **Chapter 153.** No factual change; the summary above stands. **The standing problem is said for the fourth time here and the entry says four.**
4. **Chapter 154.** The registrar's tenure is nine years in the room and thirty-one in the building, the other twenty-two being a room upstairs with a bell in it. **Her rule is given inside about eleven seconds and the entry's opening label says seconds, not words.** The wrong shelf number is found out about three weeks later, not on a named Wednesday.
5. **Chapter 155.** **Iven Sore's count is the hand column and not the authority column**, and he says out loud that he will not argue with a woman who has stood in front of that table for four years and has asked twice. **On the Tuesday afternoon she refused to read six lines and read the four column headings instead, and then read the fourth one twice.**
6. **Chapter 156.** The interval is seven days, not two, and she put her own name on a page a week later. **The four hundred and thirty-two cards are in about nine places in one district, one each, and not in her coat. There was never a corridor conversation between him and her**: she says she read a leaflet about a practice that belongs to nobody, read it twice, believes it, and worked out afterwards that the leaflet and the page were about the same thing. **Her sentence on the half landing claims no word count. Bea Nunn's book has not been opened in about two months.**
7. **Chapter 157.** The motive is arithmetic and every figure in it is right, and **he does not say the word tear before he does it**; the slip goes in the tray, and it is the only thing in the tray, and then it is in four pieces in the bin by the bell. The body prose is third person throughout. **She says she has known him since about half past twelve last night, not eleven o'clock.** Two words went in *two boxes*.
8. **Chapter 158.** The room is forty folding chairs with about nine of them empty and about thirty people in it. **The standing problem is the fifth time and the chapter says five.** **The man of forty-four does not restate Doreen Abbiss's discovery in her own words**; the entry records that he did not say it, that she said it four months ago in a corridor to a woman who types things, and that Marek Senn does not know whether he has it and is not going to ask him tonight. **Peter Amado is paid for the seventh time for this one and his two microphone asks are three events.** Five documents including the levy note go to eleven thousand premises, with about four thousand leaflets going with it into nine hundred rooms and about four hundred more in the room for the next one.
9. **Chapter 159.** One fix, in the entry only: it is now a record of the envelope, the eleven instalments of eleven pounds out of a mailbox four streets away, three weeks of not moving it, the cost she named herself, and the customer putting it inside a coat and saying nothing, which was correct. **The chapter title repeats Chapter 146's *Four Feet Of Shop* and that is a deliberate bookend thirteen chapters apart.**
10. **Chapter 160.** Fourteen lines in pencil on the back of the sheet, upside down relative to the front, in the space where the conditions go, and it is not a condition; nine in Bower Street, three in the second building, two in a stairwell in Saltmarket; **the fourth building came up with nothing on any of its doors, the first time in nineteen years.** The plate says 2-14 and **every leaflet in the rack in that room says 2-07** — not the plan, which nobody has stood in front of since the spring, and not her personal list, which made the discovery self-referential; the rack is the building's own record and it is the same chain Chapter 152 puts it on. The ring file holds **about a thousand condition sheets**, one a Thursday for nineteen years, and she cleans about four hundred of the nine hundred plates. **The basement origin is four years, not nineteen, and "since before I came" belongs to the room being 2-07 and to nothing else.** **In the body and in entry 163 the elapsed interval is three weeks and the forward one is about four weeks**, and **entry 163 no longer attributes to a corridor conversation on the Thursday of week forty-seven words that are on no page in Chapter 152** — it records what Chapter 152 says, which is that she said there was a thing and would not say what it was until the Thursday after, and that the sleep claim belongs to Doreen Abbiss in Chapter 157 and not to Ines Kolar. His Monday note is italic and not bold, the corridor became a table on the ground floor, and the *about eleven people a week* refrain carries its consequence once.

### The measured figures after all three passes, reproducible from the ten chapter files with the strict matchers printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md`

**31,534 words. Raw instances of the word *about*: 349, 11.07 per thousand. True hedges: 218, 6.91 per thousand, against the 6.80 this batch inherited. Bold spans: 211, 6.69 per thousand, worst chapter 8.08**, against ceilings of 10.75 and 12.4. The eight-word overlap between each load-book entry and its own chapter is **108, 93, 259, 279, 266, 230, 200, 170, 249 and 228** — a mean of 208 and a range of 93 to 279, all ten inside the family of 38 to 284 that Volume 03 ran in, with none above it, **which is the first time in this batch's history that has been true.** Every earlier set of figures for this batch is superseded and none of them was deleted: 29,758 words and 182 true at 6.12, then 31,247 and 213 at 6.82, then 31,287 and 213 at 6.81, then 31,287 and 218 at 6.95 with 312 bold at 9.94. **The counts moved because the prose moved, not because the matcher did.**

# VOLUME 06 CLOSE — the dated hand-off, written after Chapter 300, and it SUPERSEDES every `VOLUME 06 — MOVEMENT` block in these five files for anything about what Volume 07 inherits

**Volume 06 is CLOSED on the page at Chapter 300 and the load-book run ends at entry 303, continuous with the fiftieth at Chapter 1, with no duplicate and no gap. The account is `workspace/volume-06/batch-0006/VOLUME-CLOSE.md` and the calendar is `workspace/volume-06/ARITHMETIC-AND-CALENDAR.md`, whose section 6 is now written and is a close section. `outline/series.md` was not edited by any phase of this volume. `outline/volume-06.md` was not edited by this phase, though the close records where the prose departed from it in its section 3, and the six deviations there are decisions and not defects. This phase wrote no prose, edited no chapter, and created no new phase directory and no marker.**


**The per-chapter entries for all fifty chapters of Volume 06 are in `state/continuity.md`'s five `VOLUME 06 — MOVEMENT` blocks and, in the form they were written, in the five batch summaries. What follows is the close's account of the arc, the deviations, and the volume's figures, and it does not replace the per-chapter detail.**

**Movement I, Chapters 251 to 260, weeks 89 to 91.** A page into a ring binder unread. A printed standard on a wall in a district office, real, numbered, dated eleven years, never withdrawn. A sentence said in a room is nine people, repeated in three buildings and thanked in one. A practice card with four of the five parts of a technique in it. **The rule invoked correctly in about nine seconds by a clerk of forty-four, about nine hundred cards out of use in about four days on four days of leave nobody authorised, and it changes nothing.** A company refused in writing, and the cost of the no is that the only findable person in that building signed it. A hearing set, with two faults, one of which is a secondment. **The close: a fourteenth line on the hardboard in fourteen words, unsigned, by a woman of twenty-four, with the cost named out loud first** (260). Milestone 1 is in 259, paid, in public, and he is wrong in nine words and does not argue.

**Movement II, Chapters 261 to 270, weeks 92 to 94.** A hearing in a room in Crown Terrace producing twelve words — a room cannot ask to be heard again and a person has to — and owed to nobody. **The Exchange, twelfth sitting, the book shut, the reason new and about a room** (262). A delivery stoppage costing a woman of nineteen a friendship for a term, named at a sink. A woman of thirty-four asked for the fourth time for a practice's ending to be written down and refused in four words. About nine hundred doors stopped being a number of objects and became about six weeks of one person's year. **A plate in a service cupboard in Nine Bridges, found by a man of thirty-four who works nights, who did not touch it and wrote down that he did not, and the fifth part is absent and there is no instruction on the plate by which a person determines they are finished** (267). **The close: a fifteenth line at half past one in the morning in fourteen words, unsigned, with a space left where a name would go, and the cost is that he is the only person in this case who can now be found at a door** (270).

**Movement III, Chapters 271 to 280, weeks 95 to 98.** A request in a corridor (271). **The midpoint, Chapter 275: eleven minutes made fourteen years ago, a woman of fifty-three, a recorder stuck four years in a basement, a man of thirty-four who opened the door, the words *the Crown Clause* in her own mouth eleven seconds in, and the volume's one panel, which is the first in six volumes that agrees with a person** (275). A secondment that keeps a record alive. A woman of about sixty-eight asked to take down a sign that is not true, and did not, which is the first time anybody in that room has watched a person decline (272). A family right about a care-link. A rota nobody made. **The close: a sixteenth line that is a refusal, and costs its writer the opposite of what the fifteenth cost its writer** (280).

**Movement IV, Chapters 281 to 290, weeks 98 to 100.** A hearing that produces one thing and not a verdict (281). A subscription sold by a man of forty-one who gave his name once, in one word, unwritten, and the reasoning is better than the product (287). A council suspended in writing on the ground of an emergency, which cost a room four people and a day room, and which is right about one thing nobody improved on (284). **The heat comes back on the Wednesday of week ninety-nine because a landlord put it on, and the notice comes nine days later, and the number of names on the lease is the number of names that now cost more** (285, 290). **The word said out loud once, to one person, uncorrected, and nobody thanked him, and the not reporting is hers** (289). **The close: a seventeenth line, signed, about being findable, and a person with an ordinary job is not a post** (290).

**Movement V, Chapters 291 to 300, weeks 101 to 108.** **A procedure assembled from about nine things other people did, and he is not on it, and the writing of it down was refused by the man who assembled it** (291). **A restriction and not a repeal, named in a room, and about four hundred people can still invoke the rule** (292). The first Friday rota of the run and the gap settled for one Friday and not for the reason anybody would want (293). **A civic sequence in three districts, a partial cascade, nobody dies, eleven buildings lose something for a day, and nobody in this case knows who ran it and no name is attached to anything** (294, 296). **The room in Crown Terrace, in which a woman of twenty-four describes a room off a service road in her own words for about eleven minutes and a man of twenty-two sits at the far end of the table for two hours and ten minutes and does not speak** (295). The word said out loud once in a public room to about nine people, and nobody thanked him and nobody wrote it down and the number is not smaller this morning (297). **Four people, four findable pieces, him not one of them, and the true consequence: next time it will not be the same four, and on the Tuesday nothing happens** (298). The Exchange, fifteenth sitting, the book opens, one line in about nine seconds, and four minutes that are in no file at all (299). **The close: the sixteenth sitting, the book shut, the reason is about a room and is the first in nineteen years, and at about half past seven a woman of sixty-one walks nine miles into a room where nobody knew her and writes the eighteenth line of the wall in nineteen words and signs it, and the forward question is written once on a service road and is not answered** (300).

**THE SIX DEVIATIONS FROM `outline/volume-06.md`, ALL DECISIONS AND NONE REVERSED BY THIS CLOSE.** The hidden sequence is delivered without her name on it (294). The compromised plate is not a forwarder (267). **The cascade stops because four people were each findable and he was not one of them, which is the answer to the question this case has been asking for about four years and is not the answer to the one it should have been asking, and the volume says so out loud in one sentence in Chapter 298 and does not improve on it** (298). The volume wrote an eighteenth line and the outline provides for seventeen (300). The man of thirty-four who works nights is a count in all fifty chapters and is named at the close. The outline's four new people are named and a fifth is named who is not in the outline and a sixth gets a surname and the first-year of nineteen is a count.

**THE VOLUME'S MEASURED FIGURES, RE-RUN ON THE FIFTY FILES BY THE CLOSE.** **155,086 words** — 27,269, 27,759, 33,870, 29,725 and 36,463. **1,061 bold spans at 6.841 per thousand, worst chapter 11.60 at Chapter 253**, against ceilings of 10.75 and 12.4, both met. **Hedge: 3,276 raw, 265 prepositional, 3,011 true at 19.42 per thousand case-sensitive; 3,392 raw, 266 prepositional, 3,126 true at 20.16 case-insensitively; one hundred and sixteen capitalised instances excluded, seventy sentence-initial.** **Eight-word overlap on the reproduced matcher: 60.3, 80.9, 155.7, 153.6, 60.6, volume mean 102.2.** **Apparatus share: 43.69, 45.81, 50.61, 54.85, 47.31 per cent, volume 48.57.** **The per-paragraph instrument, run over all three hundred and ninety-three load-book paragraphs in the volume: one hundred and fourteen share nothing with the body above them, a hundred and four share one to nine, fifty-seven share ten to seventeen, a hundred and seventeen share eighteen to seventy, and one — Chapter 288's record of the reason the book was shut — shares ninety-three. Thirty-six are at forty or above and all thirty-six are listed at section 10 of the close, with the six that are a form named separately from the rest.**

**WHAT THE CLOSE FLAGGED AND DID NOT FIX, IN ONE LINE EACH, AND NONE OF THE NINE WAS REPAIRED.** The calendar had three wrong rows and not four, and Chapter 290 is not one of them. Chapter 290's alleged two-day slip is not a slip. The drafting-reference construction is in seven chapters of the prose and not six. Of the two day numbers, one is in the calendar and is correct and one is in a spent prompt and is real. **Chapter 300's body carries a near-duplicate pair of paragraphs at lines 137 and 139, the same class of defect that Movement IV's review found in Chapter 289's load book, and it is in the close of the volume.** The words *we*, *our* and *us* are in eighteen of the fifty chapters and not all fifty. *Run of days* is a banned synonym in Movement V and an accepted form in Movements III and IV, fourteen lines in eleven chapters. **Chapters 262 and 300 each claim to be the first time in nineteen years the reason was about a room and not a person, and both are on the page.** And three published figures do not reproduce. **Owner for the nine: the next repair pass over Movements II and V, the next pass over the Movement III prompt and the outline, and this close for the calendar, which it has already done.**

# VOLUME 06 CLOSE — REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and what a reader of Chapters 291 to 300 now has

**No chapter was rewritten and no scene, beat, day, name, character or entry number was touched. One figure was corrected in place in each of the last ten chapters of the volume, and it is the same figure in all ten: the age of the wall's seventeenth line.**

## THE SEVENTEENTH LINE, corrected in Chapters 291 to 300, and it is a load-book figure in all ten

**The seventeenth line was written in Chapter 290, on the Friday of week 100, which is day 590. The ten chapters that follow it are on days 593, 595, 597, 600, 602, 603, 604, 607, 616 and 644. Every one of the ten figures was computed from day 592 — the Sunday of week 100, and not a chapter of this volume — and every one of the ten was two days low. All ten are corrected in place, one figure in each chapter, in the *Conditions and docket* entry that carries the four wall ages.**

**The other three series were walked value by value across the same ten chapters while this was being fixed and all thirty of their values are right: the fourteen from day 526, the fifteen from 547 and the sixteen from 572.** The corrected seventeen now runs 3, 5, 7, 10, 12, 13, 14, 17, 26, 54 and every step in it matches the gap in days between the two chapters, including the twenty-eight days from Chapter 299 to Chapter 300, which is what made the original error visible as a thirty-day jump across a twenty-eight-day gap.

**All ten figures stand in the load book and none is in the body.** Each is on one line of the *Conditions and docket* entry and nowhere else in its chapter, so there is no second witness to any of them and the body neither corroborates the load book nor contradicts it. **That is a property of this volume's form and not a defect in it — the wall's ages are apparatus — but it is the reason a wrong figure here survives every pass that reads the prose.**

**Nothing else moved, and the arithmetic of the volume survived the repair whole.** Each of the ten chapters is unchanged in word count and in bold-span count: 3,479/20, 3,476/19, 3,002/12, 3,395/15, 5,143/35, 3,401/23, 3,344/24, 3,211/26, 3,191/21 and 4,821/26, before and after. Movement V is **36,463 words** and the volume is **155,086**, and the load-book overlap moved by **0.0** in every one of the five movements and **0.0** for the volume. **No figure this repository publishes about Volume 06 needed re-publishing, and that is the condition under which a closed volume may be touched at all.**

## Chapter 300 — The Sixteenth Sitting (load-book entry 303, the Wednesday of week 108, day 644)

**Unchanged but for the two figures, both in the load book.** The *Conditions and docket* entry now reads *the sixteen seventy-two, and the seventeen fifty-four*, and the *Nine miles, eighteen lines, and the eighteenth* entry now reads *Age of the newest before this evening: fifty-four days.* The eight wall figures at that chapter, all walked on the day map: the room **282** — the only one of the eight that also appears in the body, twice — the card in its rail **286**, the twelve lines **202**, the thirteen **153**, the fourteen **118**, the fifteen **97**, the sixteen **72**, the seventeen **54**, and the eighteen **0**, written that evening.

**What the chapter is, unchanged.** The sixteenth sitting of the Exchange, four weeks to the day after the fifteenth, the book shut again, and the reason given is **the first in nineteen years that has been about a room rather than about a person** — which is also Chapter 262's claim, sixteen weeks earlier, in the same hand, and the two cannot both be the first of their kind and neither is repaired. The holder of the Exchange is sixty-one and has never been in the room off the service road before that evening, and she walks about nine miles, and she writes the **eighteenth line** in nineteen words in about nine seconds with a pen off the counter, and she names the cost aloud first in four items, and the fourth of them is that about four people in this city will be able to find a woman of sixty-one at a building with a plate on the outside of it. The telephone moved from fifty-four to fifty-seven and the book stayed at forty-eight. The two things nobody in that room raised are the printed rule and the Continuity Office, and the count has now been announced twenty-one times, of which sixteen are the sixteen Wednesdays this book holds and five predate it on a sheet she has never shown anybody.
